Matrix-product operator dualities in integrable lattice models
Yuan Miao, Andras Molnar, Nick G. Jones

Abstract
. Matrix-product operators (MPOs) appear throughout the study of integrable lattice models, notably as the transfer matrices. They can also be used as transformations to construct dualities between such models, both invertible (including unitary) and non-invertible (including discrete gauging). We analyse how the local Yang–Baxter integrable structures are modified under such dualities. We see that the R ˇ \check{R} -matrix, that appears in the baxterization approach to integrability, transforms in a simple manner. We further show for a broad class of MPOs that the usual Yang–Baxter R R -matrix satisfies a modified algebra, previously identified in the unitary case, that gives a local integrable structure underlying the commuting transfer matrices of the dual model. We illustrate these results with two case studies, analysing an invertible unitary MPO and a non-invertible MPO both applied to the canonical XXZ spin chain. The former is the cluster entangler, arising in the study of symmetry-protected topological phases, while the latter is the Kramers–Wannier duality. We show several results for MPOs with exact MPO inverses that are of independent interest.



1.
Introduction

Integrable spin chains are key to our understanding of quantum many-body systems, giving us analytical tools to explore wide-ranging phenomena
[
1
,
2
,
3
,
4
]
. For example, consider the spin-1/2 XXZ chain with Hamiltonian

(1)

\displaystyle H_{\mathrm{XXZ}}=\sum_{j=1}^{L}\frac{1}{2}\left(X_{j}X_{j+1}+Y_{j}Y_{j+1}+\Delta(\mathbb{I}+Z_{j}Z_{j+1})\right)\qquad\Delta\in\mathbb{R}\ ;

the local terms are products of the usual Pauli operators on each site, and
H_{\mathrm{XXZ}}
has a
U(1)
spin rotation symmetry generated by
\sum_{j}Z_{j}
. This model describes the transition between certain symmetry-breaking phases, as well as a gapped paramagnetic phase
[
5
]
. For
\lvert\Delta\rvert<1
it is described at low energies by the compactified free boson conformal field theory (CFT), and tuning
\Delta
allows us to explore the moduli space of this CFT on the lattice
[
6
,
7
]
.

The XXZ spin-chain is solvable using the Bethe ansatz
[
8
,
9
]
, and the underlying integrable structure is a one-parameter solution to the Yang–Baxter equation called the
R
-matrix. This solution corresponds to the six-vertex model of classical statistical mechanics
[
1
,
2
]
. From the
R
-matrix we can construct commuting matrix-product operators (MPOs, see
Fig.

1
), known as transfer matrices, from which one can derive the Hamiltonian and local conserved charges of
H_{\mathrm{XXZ}}
.
Note that even with this integrable structure, computing correlation functions remains highly non-trivial
[
3
,
10
]
.
Since transfer matrices are expressed as MPOs, much of the formalism related to integrable models can be understood in the language of tensor networks
[
11
,
12
]
.

In this work we are interested in maps or dualities between different spin chains
[
13
,
14
,
15
,
16
,
17
]
. In particular, we seek to understand the interplay between these maps and the underlying local integrable structure of the spin chain, such as the
{R}
-matrix. Many interesting examples of such dualities are known, such as the Kramers–Wannier duality in the Ising model
[
13
]
. This duality can be understood more generally as gauging the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
symmetry in a spin chain
[
18
,
19
,
20
,
21
]
or as a ‘vertex-face correspondence’ in the related classical statistical mechanical model
[
22
,
23
,
24
]
.

Importantly, the Kramers–Wannier duality can itself be expressed as an MPO
[
15
]
. We will be interested in the effect of MPO transformations applied to integrable spin chains more generally. The simplest such transformation would be a unitary MPO with bond-dimension one, i.e. a local on-site unitary transformation. These are trivial and can be easily absorbed into the physical indices of the transfer matrix. It is less clear what happens when we conjugate by a matrix-product unitary (MPU); recall that an MPU is a unitary MPO with bond-dimension greater than one. We will see that these transformations are globally very simple, but change the local integrable structure. Finally, the most interesting case that we consider are MPOs that change the spectrum of the model. These represent
non-invertible duality transformations, generalising the Kramers–Wannier duality.

The MPU case was previously explored in Ref.
[
17
]
, we expand and generalize this discussion here. In particular, we show that the MPU analysis applies also to MPOs that have an exact (i.e. finite bond-dimension) MPO inverse. We also explore the connection to Jones’ baxterization approach to integrable models
[
4
]
. Note that despite their apparent simplicity, certain MPUs are physically important and appear in the setting of symmetry-protected topological (SPT) phases. These MPUs act as SPT entanglers
[
25
,
26
]

that map trivial paramagnetic Hamiltonians to SPT Hamiltonians, where the ground states of the latter have non-trivial symmetry fractionalisation
[
27
,
28
,
29
,
30
,
31
,
32
,
33
,
34
]
. Indeed, one motivation to consider these duality transformations is to map between related integrable models with differing symmetry properties and SPT physics, and to identify integrable transitions between SPT phases.

The outline of the paper is as follows. In
Section

2
we review the Yang–Baxter (in terms of quantum group) and baxterization constructions of integrable spin chains and introduce the
R
and
\check{R}
-matrices that appear in each approach respectively. In
Section

3
we introduce three classes of dualities and give a preliminary discussion of their action. We then analyse two cases in detail, first a bond-dimension two MPU in
Section

5
and then the non-invertible Kramers–Wannier MPO in
Section

6
. The first example is representative of general features of MPO with exact MPO inverse. We outline some results for this class of MPO that are of independent interest, and then conclude our discussion of maps and dualities in
Section

7
.

Figure 1
.
A reference matrix-product operator (MPO). Each four-index tensor
M_{j}
, that may also depend on some parameters, has two physical indices (vertical lines) and two virtual indices (horizontal lines). For fixed virtual indices,
M_{i}
gives a linear transformation between physical Hilbert spaces
V_{i}\rightarrow V_{i}^{\prime}
. These are referred to as ‘quantum space’(s) in some integrability literature. Fixing the physical indices
M_{i}
gives a matrix mapping
W_{i-1}\rightarrow W_{i}
. The
V_{i}
are referred to in the literature as bond, virtual or auxilliary spaces. We will often take translation invariant MPOs acting on a fixed Hilbert space. In this case all
M_{i}=M
, all virtual spaces are isomorphic, and, taking the trace over the virtual space
V_{0}\equiv W_{j}
, we have an operator from
V^{\otimes L}
to itself.

2.
Review: key notions of integrability and Yang–Baxter equation

In this section, we present the key notions of quantum integrability in terms of MPOs. The renowned Yang–Baxter equation (YBE) can be written naturally in this language. After introducing the canonical approach to Yang–Baxter integrability, we show another algebraic approach to quantum integrability called
baxterization
. The latter approach is useful to reveal the integrable structure of models that are related by duality transformations.

Figure 2
.
‘Scattering’ YBE for
R
-matrix. The labels
1
,
2
and
3
stands for the three vector spaces in
V\otimes V\otimes V
. Arrows indicate the operators acting from right to left.

2.1.
The
R
and
\check{R}
-matrices

The
R
-matrix is a four-index tensor
R_{\alpha,\beta}^{\gamma,\delta}(u,v)
that acts on
V\otimes V
, for some ‘bond’ vector space
V
, by
R(u,v)\left|\alpha\beta\right>=\sum_{\gamma\delta}R_{\alpha,\beta}^{\gamma,\delta}(u,v)\left|\gamma\delta\right>
, and depends on two spectral parameters
u
and
v
. Abusing notation, define
R_{12}=R\otimes\mathbb{I}
,
R_{23}=\mathbb{I}\otimes R
, and
R_{13}=\sum_{i}x_{i}\otimes\mathbb{I}\otimes y_{i}
where
R=\sum_{i}x_{i}\otimes y_{i}
; then the YBE is given by
[
1
,
12
]

(2)

\displaystyle R_{12}(u,v)R_{13}(u,\xi)R_{23}(v,\xi)=R_{23}(v,\xi)R_{13}(u,\xi)R_{12}(u,v)\;.

Diagrammatically this is given in
Fig.

2
, and we call it the scattering YBE.

Alternatively, we define
\check{R}(u,v)=SR(u,v)
, where
S
is the swap operator (also known as the permutation operator)
S:V_{1}\otimes V_{2}\to V_{2}\otimes V_{1}
. The operators
\check{R}
satisfy the equation

(3)

\displaystyle\check{R}_{23}(u,v)\check{R}_{12}(u,\xi)\check{R}_{23}(v,\xi)=\check{R}_{12}(v,\xi)\check{R}_{23}(u,\xi)\check{R}_{12}(u,v)\ .

Diagrammatically this is given in
Fig.

3
, and we call it the circuit YBE. In the following we allow
\check{R}
to act on
k>2
sites, in which case we write
\check{R}_{i}
for the operator that acts on sites
[i,i+1,\dots,i+k-1]
.

Figure 3
.
‘Circuit’ YBE for
\check{{R}}
-matrix. This is
Eq.

3
where we specialize to the case where
\check{{R}}
acts on two neighbouring sites. This moreover represents
Eq.

16
when we take the representation over a tensorized Hilbert space where
\check{{\mathcal{R}}}_{j}\mapsto\check{R}_{j,j+1}
.

2.2.
Commuting transfer matrices and conserved charges

Given the set of matrices

\{R(u,v)|u,v\in\mathbb{R}\}\subseteq\mathrm{End}(V\otimes V)
, we define the
monodromy matrix

M_{0}(u,\xi)\in\mathrm{End}(V\otimes V^{\otimes L})
for every
u\in\mathbb{R}
and
\xi=(\xi_{1},\xi_{2},\dots\xi_{L})\in\mathbb{C}^{L}
as

\displaystyle M_{0}(u,\xi)=\prod_{j=L}^{1}R_{0j}(u,\xi_{j})\;.

Here we label the tensor components from
0
to
L
, and the tensor component labeled by
0
has a special role (as all operators in the product act on it); the label
0
of
M_{0}(u,\xi)
emphasizes this. Writing
R(u,\xi)=\sum_{i}x_{i}(u,\xi)\otimes y_{i}(u,\xi)
, with
x_{i}(u,\xi),y_{i}(u,\xi)\in\mathrm{End}(V)
, and keeping track of the tensor components explicitly, this expression reads

(4)

\displaystyle M_{0}(u,\xi)=\sum_{i_{1},\dots,i_{L}}\left(x_{i_{L}}(u,\xi_{L})\cdot x_{i_{L-1}}(u,\xi_{L-1})\cdots x_{i_{1}}(u,\xi_{1})\right)\otimes y_{i_{1}}(u,\xi_{1})\otimes y_{i_{2}}(u,\xi_{2})\otimes\dots\otimes y_{i_{L}}(u,\xi_{L})\;,

or, writing out the matrix components,

(5)

\displaystyle M_{0}(u,\xi)=\sum_{\bm{\alpha},\bm{\beta},\bm{\delta}}R_{\alpha_{1}\beta_{L}}^{\alpha_{2}\delta_{L}}(u,\xi_{1})R_{\alpha_{2}\beta_{L-1}}^{\alpha_{3}\delta_{L-1}}(u,\xi_{2})\dots R_{\alpha_{L}\beta_{1}}^{\alpha_{L+1}\delta_{1}}(u,\xi_{L})\,\left|\alpha_{1}\right>\left<\alpha_{L+1}\right|\otimes\left|\delta_{1}\dots\delta_{L}\right>\left<\beta_{1}\dots\beta_{L}\right|.

This operator is an MPO with open boundaries. Throughout the paper we use a graphical notation of tensor networks to depict such operators. The notation is explained in
Fig.

1
. Using this notation,
M_{0}(u,\xi)
can be depicted as

(6)

\displaystyle M_{0}(u,\xi)=\hbox to275.83pt{\vbox to52.31pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 44.01698pt\lower-17.27182pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\ ,

where the arrows on the horizontal line (the very first tensor component of
M_{0}
, labeled by
0
) emphasize that the linear operators act there from the left to right: each
R
matrix is a linear operator on
V\otimes V
, and the input indices are the bottom (maps to the top index) and the left (maps to the right index). That is,

(7)

\displaystyle R_{\alpha,\beta}^{\gamma,\delta}(u,\xi)=\hbox to88.04pt{\vbox to51pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 44.01698pt\lower-25.98734pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}

The
transfer matrix

T(u,\xi)\in\mathrm{End}\big(V^{\otimes L}\big)
with parameters
\xi=(\xi_{1},\xi_{2},\dots\xi_{L})\in\mathbb{C}^{L}
is then defined as the trace over the first tensor component of this operator (the one labeled by
0
). The resulting operator acts on the Hilbert space
V^{\otimes L}
and is given by

(8)

\displaystyle T(u,\xi)=\mathrm{Tr}_{0}\left(M_{0}(u,\xi)\right)=\mathrm{Tr}_{0}\left(\prod_{j=L}^{1}R_{0j}(u,\xi_{j})\right)\;.

Just as above, we can express this operator as

\displaystyle T(u,\xi)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i_{1}\dots i_{L}}\mathrm{Tr}\,\big(x_{i_{L}}(u,\xi_{L})\cdot x_{i_{L-1}}(u,\xi_{L-1})\cdots x_{i_{1}}(u,\xi_{L})\big)\cdot y_{i_{1}}(u,\xi_{1})\otimes y_{i_{2}}(u,\xi_{2})\otimes\dots\otimes y_{i_{L}}(u,\xi_{L}),

(9)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\bm{\alpha},\bm{\beta},\bm{\delta}}R_{\alpha_{1}\beta_{L}}^{\alpha_{2}\delta_{L}}(u,\xi_{1})R_{\alpha_{2}\beta_{L-1}}^{\alpha_{3}\delta_{L-1}}(u,\xi_{2})\dots R_{\alpha_{L}\beta_{1}}^{\alpha_{1}\delta_{1}}(u,\xi_{L})\,\left|\delta_{1}\dots\delta_{L}\right>\left<\beta_{1}\dots\beta_{L}\right|\;.

The operator
T(u,\xi)
has the defining form of an MPO with periodic boundary conditions (as in
Fig.

1
,
W_{0}=\dots W_{n}=V_{0}
and
V_{i}^{\prime}=V_{i}
),

(10)

\displaystyle T(u,\xi)=\hbox to250.79pt{\vbox to55.73pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 40.03368pt\lower-20.68613pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\ .

From the YBE, we have

(11)

\displaystyle R_{ab}(u,v)M_{a}(u,\xi)M_{b}(v,\xi)=M_{b}(v,\xi)M_{a}(u,\xi)R_{ab}(u,v)\;.

We assume that generically
R_{ab}(u,v)
is invertible and so, taking the partial trace, the transfer matrices commute with each other:

(12)

\displaystyle\left[T(u,\xi),T(v,\xi)\right]=0\;.

The logarithm of the transfer matrix can be used to generate local conserved quantities
[
3
,
2
,
4
]
. In many integrable models, including the XXZ chain, the
R
matrix is normalized so that
R(0)=S
(and
\check{R}=\mathbb{I}
). In such a case, taking a homogeneous limit
\xi_{n}\rightarrow 0
, we have that
Q_{n}
, defined by

(13)

\displaystyle Q_{n}=\left(\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}u}\right)^{n-1}\log T(u)\Big|_{u=0}\ ,

are mutually commuting charges, with
Q_{2}
being the Hamiltonian of the integrable model and where
T(u):=\lim_{\xi_{n}\to 0}T(u,\xi)
.

More generally, local tensors of the MPO monodromy matrix are referred to as Lax operators and denoted by
L(u)
(with
\xi
dependence suppressed). In
Eq.

8
we have
L(u)=R(u,\xi_{j})
, i.e. we simply identify two indices of the
R
-matrix to correspond to the physical indices. Models with transfer matrices constructed in this way are called fundamental integrable models. More generally Lax operators are intertwined by an
R
-matrix in the RLL relation:

(14)

One way to arrive at solutions to this equation, beyond fundamental integrable models, is to use the fusion relations of the
R
-matrix
[
35
]
. One can construct Lax operators with tensored representations, for example the higher-spin representations in the
U_{q}(\hat{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2})
case
1
1

1

The XXZ
R
-matrix is related to the spin-
1/2
evaluation representation of
U_{q}(\hat{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2})

[
36
]
.
, sharing the same intertwiner
R
. In this article we focus instead on models associated with the fundamental representation, and their MPO duals.

Assuming that
R
is invertible, the RLL relation allows us to prove the commutativity of the transfer matrices exactly as above: one inserts
RR^{-1}
on any bond, pulls
R
through all Lax operators reversing their order, and then the remaining
R^{-1}R
can be removed. In fundamental integrable models, keeping track of indices, the YBE
is
the RLL relation. The RLL relation is, however, more general than the YBE.

We remark that mathematically the solutions to the YBE (
2
) can be classified and obtained systematically from the representation theory of quantum groups
[
35
,
36
]
. We will not focus on this point in the rest of the paper.

2.3.
Baxterization

Alternatively to the above, Yang–Baxter integrable models can be constructed through a procedure called baxterization. This is summarized in Jones’ seminal work
[
37
]
, we review the key points here.

The framework of baxterization is established in purely algebraic terms. Let us consider a unital associative algebra
\mathcal{A}
over the field
\mathbb{C}
. We assume that there are elements of the algebra
\mathcal{A}
,

(15)

\displaystyle\{x_{j}^{(m)}\}\subset\mathcal{A}\,,\quad j\in\{1,2,\dots,K\}\,,\quad m\in\{1,2,\dots,n\}\;,

satisfying certain relations. The aim of the baxterization procedure is to find algebraic solutions,
\check{\mathcal{R}}
, to the circuit YBE

(16)

\displaystyle\check{\mathcal{R}}_{j+1}(u,v)\check{\mathcal{R}}_{j}(u,\xi)\check{\mathcal{R}}_{j+1}(v,\xi)=\check{\mathcal{R}}_{j}(v,\xi)\check{\mathcal{R}}_{j+1}(u,\xi)\check{\mathcal{R}}_{j}(u,v)\;,

where

(17)

\displaystyle\check{\mathcal{R}}_{j}(u,v)=\sum_{m=1}^{n}f_{n}(u,v)x^{(n)}_{j}\;,

with certain complex functions
f_{n}(u,v)
.

There is no assumption on the exact form of the
\check{\mathcal{R}}
, nor the space it acts upon
2
2

2

We use the terminology
\check{\mathcal{R}}
-matrix to stand for the
abstract

\check{\mathcal{R}}
operator.
. The circuit YBE for the
\check{R}
-matrix, given in
Eq.

3
, gives concrete Hilbert space solutions (derived from standard
R
-matrices) to the more abstract
Eq.

16
.
Moreover,
Fig.

3
is a specific realization of (
16
) when the
\check{\mathcal{R}}
-matrix acts on two neighboring sites of a tensor product Hilbert space.

In order to construct the transfer matrix in this approach, we further require that

(18)

\displaystyle\left[\check{\mathcal{R}}_{j}(u_{1},v_{1}),\check{\mathcal{R}}_{k}(u_{2},v_{2})\right]=0,\quad\quad|j-k|\geq 2\;.

Then we are able to define the
baxterized
transfer matrix following
[
37
]
,

(19)

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}(u,\xi)=\check{\mathcal{R}}_{K}(u,\xi)\check{\mathcal{R}}_{K-1}(u,\xi)\cdots\check{\mathcal{R}}_{2}(u,\xi)\check{\mathcal{R}}_{1}(u,\xi)\;.

By recursively applying the YBE (
16
) to the product of two transfer matrices, we have

(20)

\displaystyle\check{\mathcal{R}}_{K}(v,\xi)\check{\mathcal{R}}_{K}(u,v)\check{\mathcal{R}}_{K}^{-1}(u,\xi)\mathcal{T}(u,\xi)\mathcal{T}(v,\xi)=\mathcal{T}(v,\xi)\mathcal{T}(u,\xi)\check{\mathcal{R}}_{1}^{-1}(u,\xi)\check{\mathcal{R}}_{1}(u,v)\check{\mathcal{R}}_{1}(v,\xi)\;.

“Thus up to boundary terms the transfer matrices commute”
[
37
]
. In many examples, it is possible to construct truly commuting transfer matrices from the baxterized transfer matrices, dealing more carefully with the boundary terms.
For cases where
\check{\mathcal{R}}\mapsto\check{R}
, derived from a standard
R
-matrix, then the baxterized transfer matrices (
19
) give rise
3
3

3

With appropriate simple manipulations, as they are not strictly equal.
to the standard transfer matrix (
8
).

As for the solutions to (
16
), there are two renowned examples: 1. solutions with the Temperley–Lieb (TL) algebra
[
38
]
and 2. solutions with the Birman–Murakami–Wenzl (BMW) algebra
[
39
,
40
]
. By taking different representations of the Hecke algebra
[
41
,
42
,
43
,
44
,
45
]
, for example, in the TL case, we have the 6-vertex model (XXZ spin chain), the solid-on-solid (SOS) model, the alternating fundamental–anti-fundamental
SU(n)
chain,
N
-state Potts model and more. We thus obtain a variety of different models, whose integrable structures are all described by the TL algebra.
We consider here the TL algebra defined in a field
\mathbb{C}[\beta]
with
\beta=q+q^{-1}
, where the parameter
q\in\mathbb{C}^{\times}
. When the fugacity parameters
\beta
of two TL algebras coincide, the spectra of the quantum Hamiltonian as the sum of all TL generators share a similar structure, though the degeneracies might differ
[
46
]
. Some of them are more explicitly related by dualities, such as the 6-vertex model and the SOS model, where certain eigenvectors can be mapped to each other through the duality transformation
[
47
,
23
,
24
]
. Therefore, the approach of baxterization gives us a powerful way to study integrable models that share the same algebraic structure.

We focus here on the case of chromatic algebra with
3
-colouring
[
48
,
49
,
50
]
, which is closely related to the
SO(3)
BMW algebra at root of unity
q=e^{\mathrm{i}\pi/3}
.
This chromatic algebra with
3
-colouring is defined by the generators
\{1,S_{j},P_{j}\}
with
j\in\{1,2,\cdots,K\}
. They satisfy the following relations
[
50
]

(21)

\displaystyle S_{j}^{2}=1-P_{j}\,,\quad P_{j}^{2}=P_{j}\,,\quad S_{j}P_{j}=0\,,\quad S_{j}S_{j\pm 1}S_{j}=P_{j}S_{j\pm 1}P_{j}=0\;.

Additional relations can be derived from the ones above. Meanwhile, a ‘periodic boundary condition’ is assumed, i.e.
S_{K+1}=S_{1}
and
P_{K+1}=P_{1}
.
Using the generators of the chromatic algebra, we define the
\check{\mathcal{R}}
-matrix as

(22)

\displaystyle\check{\mathcal{R}}_{j}(u;\mu)=\mathbb{I}-P_{j}+\frac{\sin u}{\sin\mu}S_{j}+\frac{\sin(u+\mu)}{\sin\mu}P_{j}\;,

where
\mu\in\mathbb{C}
is a fixed parameter.

Using the relations (
21
), the
\check{\mathcal{R}}
-matrix satisfies the YBE (
16
)

(23)

\displaystyle\check{\mathcal{R}}_{j+1}(u-v;\mu)\check{\mathcal{R}}_{j}(u;\mu)\check{\mathcal{R}}_{j+1}(v;\mu)=\check{\mathcal{R}}_{j}(v;\mu)\check{\mathcal{R}}_{j+1}(u;\mu)\check{\mathcal{R}}_{j}(u-v;\mu)\;;

that is, for each fixed value
\mu
these operators provide a solution to the YBE (
16
) via setting
\check{\mathcal{R}}(u,v)=\check{\mathcal{R}}(u-v;\mu)
.

As shown in
[
50
]
, the
\check{R}
-matrices of the XXZ spin chain (and three other integrable models) form a representation of the chromatic algebra of
3
-colouring, which we will exploit in
Section

6
.

3.
Dualities between lattice models

In this paper, we focus on different duality transformations over integrable spin chains, where the integrability is preserved but the local structures may be modified. The duality transformations act on the physical indices, as do to the Hamiltonian operator and the
\check{R}
-matrices.

To begin with, we classify the duality transformations into three different categories,

(1)

Invertible
and
on-site
duality transformations.

(2)

Invertible
and
non-on-site
duality transformations. We restrict this (a priori extremely broad) class to those that can be expressed as translation-invariant MPOs with bond dimension
\chi\geq 2
.

(3)

Non-invertible
duality transformations. Again, we restrict this class to those that can be expressed as MPOs with finite bond dimension. Physically relevant examples are often related to the gauging of a discrete symmetry of the Hamiltonian.

We consider the case when the Hilbert space of the quantum integrable spin chain is
V_{1}\otimes V_{2}\otimes\cdots\otimes V_{L}
, where
V_{n}
,
n\in\{1,2,\dots,L\}
is a finite-dimensional vector space. Translation-invariance, possibly after blocking sites, allows us to vary
L
in a given model and to take the thermodynamic limit.

3.1.
Invertible and on-site duality transformations

The invertible and on-site duality transformation can be realized by considering the operator

(24)

\displaystyle U=\prod_{j=1}^{L}\mathbf{u}_{j}\;,\quad\mathbf{u}_{j}\mathbf{u}_{j}^{-1}=1\;,

where
\mathbf{u}_{j}
is an invertible local operator
4
4

4

In principle,
\mathbf{u}_{j}
can vary with
j
. However, we focus on the translation-invariant case so that momentum remains a conserved quantity and the dependence on
L
is straightforward.
acting non-trivially on
V_{j}
only.

We define the transformed monodromy matrix and the transformed transfer matrix as

(25)

\displaystyle\tilde{M}_{0}(u,\xi)=UM_{0}(u,\xi)U^{-1},\quad\tilde{T}(u,\xi)=\mathrm{Tr}_{0}\left(UM_{0}(u,\xi)U^{-1}\right)=UT(u,\xi)U^{-1}\;.

It is straightforward to observe that the relation (
11
) remains the same,

(26)

\displaystyle R_{ab}(u,v)\tilde{M}_{a}(u,\xi)\tilde{M}_{b}(v,\xi)=\tilde{M}_{b}(v,\xi)\tilde{M}_{a}(u,\xi)R_{ab}(u,v)\;.

Therefore, the transformed transfer matrices are also mutually commuting for all values of the spectral parameters,

(27)

\displaystyle\left[\tilde{T}(u,\xi),\tilde{T}(v,\xi)\right]=0\;.

The integrable structure remains the same, since the
R
-matrix that intertwines two monodromy matrices is the same. Naturally the
\check{R}
-matrix continues to satisfy the circuit YBE.

3.2.
Invertible with MPO inverse and not-on-site MPO duality transformations

The second class comprise MPOs of the form

(28)

\displaystyle U=\mathrm{tr}\left(\prod_{j=L}^{1}u_{j}\right)\;,\quad u_{j}=\sum_{\alpha,\beta}(\mathbf{u}_{j})_{\alpha,\beta}\otimes\left|\alpha\right>\left<\beta\right|\;,

such that there exists a second MPO
U^{-1}
, with local tensor
u^{\prime}_{j}
, such that
UU^{-1}=\mathbb{I}
on the physical Hilbert space. When
U
is unitary, the MPO is an MPU, and the local tensor
(\mathbf{u^{\prime}}_{j})_{\alpha,\beta}=(\mathbf{u}_{j}^{\dagger})_{\alpha,\beta}
where we take the complex conjugate and transpose the physical indices (the corresponding tensor is denoted
\overline{u}_{j}
in the graphical notation).

In general, non-on-site transformations do not generate interesting dualities since they will transform local conserved charges to non-local operators. However, for any invertible MPO with MPO inverse, we have the result that local operators are transformed to local operators
[
51
]
, see
Section

7.1.2
. (One manifestation of this is the result that translation-invariant MPU are equivalent to quantum cellular automata
[
52
,
53
]
.) This means that the transformed model remains physical. Moreover, if we are interested in Hermitian Hamiltonians, it is natural to focus on the MPU case. This is explored in
Section

5
. However, our key observations hold also in the broader family specified. Note also that this class of dualities preserves the spectrum of the integrable model.

Because MPO transformations
\mathbf{U}
with MPO inverse preserve locality, the integrable structure of the
\check{R}
-matrix remains the same:

(29)

\displaystyle\check{\mathsf{R}}_{j}(u,v)\check{\mathsf{R}}_{j+1}(u,\xi)\check{\mathsf{R}}_{j}(v,\xi)=\check{\mathsf{R}}_{j+1}(v,\xi)\check{\mathsf{R}}_{j}(u,\xi)\check{\mathsf{R}}_{j+1}(u,v)\;,

where

(30)

\displaystyle\check{\mathsf{R}}_{j}(u,v)=U\check{R}_{j}(u,v)U^{-1}\;.

In contrast, the integrable structure in terms of the
R
-matrix is dramatically modified. Indeed, while we maintain a family of mutually commuting transfer matrices, we no longer expect to be able to find an
R
-matrix satisfying the YBE. However, the local structure of the MPO transformation allows us to define a modified RLL relation (also called the
Yang–Baxter algebra
(YBA), see
[
17
]
). This allows us to intertwine the transformed
L
operators as long as we then contract with additional
L
operators on either side. Since we do exactly that in constructing the transfer matrix, the modified RLL relation is a local structure underlying the global commutativity of the transfer matrices.

3.3.
Non-invertible MPO duality transformations

Non-invertible duality transformations, even when given by exact MPOs, do not necessarily preserve the locality of general local operators
[
17
]
. Here we focus on non-invertible duality transformations that correspond to the gauging of certain discrete symmetries of the Hamiltonian (and
\check{R}
-matrix). Symmetric operators remain local after gauging, and in particular, the Hamiltonian and
\check{R}
-matrix remain local after the duality transformation. This is crucial for integrability.

Suppose that the non-invertible duality transformation is realized by the operator
\mathcal{D}
. We have the following relation,

(31)

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}\check{R}_{j}(u,v)=\check{\mathsf{R}}_{j}(u,v)\mathcal{D}\;,

where the
\check{R}
-matrix of the dual model is local and given by
\check{\mathsf{R}}_{j}(u,v)
. The algebraic relations between
\check{R}_{j}(u,v)
remain the same for the dual
\check{R}
-matrix, hence the circuit-type Yang–Baxter equations are still satisfied

(32)

\displaystyle\check{\mathsf{R}}_{j}(u,v)\check{\mathsf{R}}_{j+1}(u,w)\check{\mathsf{R}}_{j}(v,w)=\check{\mathsf{R}}_{j+1}(v,w)\check{\mathsf{R}}_{j}(u,w)\check{\mathsf{R}}_{j+1}(u,v)\;.

In another word, Jones’ baxterization applies for the dual model as well!

It is not obvious how the transfer matrix of the dual model can be constructed, since the operator
\mathcal{D}
is
non-invertible
. Similarly to the boundary terms in baxterization, the boundary conditions of the transfer matrix must be compatible with the non-invertible duality transformation, i.e. the transfer matrix with the desired boundary condition must be symmetric under the discrete symmetry
[
54
]
(these are known as
topological
boundaries). In general, it is not guaranteed that an integrable transfer matrix for the dual model exists, especially in the presence of non-trivial topological boundaries.

In
Section

7
we expand upon the observations summarized here. Before doing so, we study concrete examples of the duality transformations introduced above that illustrate the general features. For this purpose, it is helpful to have a particular integrable model in mind. In
Sections

5
and
6
we demonstrate different duality transformations over a fundamental quantum integrable model, the spin-
\frac{1}{2}
XXZ spin chain, also known as the 6-vertex model in the statistical mechanics literature. The purpose of the following short section is to fix the notation and clarify the different integrable structures appearing in this model.

4.
The spin-
\frac{1}{2}
XXZ spin chain

We start with defining the
R
-matrix of the XXZ spin chain (6-vertex model)

(33)

\displaystyle\mathbf{R}(u;\mu)=\begin{pmatrix}\sin(u+\mu)&{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}.}&{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}.}&{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}.}\\
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}.}&\sin u&\sin\mu&{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}.}\\
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}.}&\sin\mu&\sin u&{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}.}\\
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}.}&{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}.}&{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}.}&\sin(u+\mu)\end{pmatrix}\;,

which satisfies the YBE

(34)

\displaystyle\mathbf{R}_{12}(u-v;\mu)\mathbf{R}_{13}(u;\mu)\mathbf{R}_{23}(v;\mu)=\mathbf{R}_{23}(v;\mu)\mathbf{R}_{13}(u;\mu)\mathbf{R}_{12}(u-v;\mu)\;,

where the operator defined on the two sides of this equation acts on three tensored two-dimensional Hilbert spaces labeled by
1,2,3
,
V_{1}\otimes V_{2}\otimes V_{3}=(\mathbb{C}^{2})^{\otimes 3}
.

The parameter
\mu
should be considered as a fixed parameter of the model, which is essentially the anisotropy parameter of the XXZ spin chain

(35)

\displaystyle\Delta=\cos\mu\;,

while the spectral parameter
u\in\mathbb{C}
can be chosen to take any value. The
\check{R}
-matrix can be obtained by acting the permutation operator (or swap) of two spin-
\frac{1}{2}
s on the
R
-matrix, i.e.

(36)

\displaystyle\check{\mathbf{R}}_{1,2}(u;\mu)=\mathbf{S}_{1,2}\mathbf{R}_{1,2}(u;\mu)=\begin{pmatrix}\sin(u+\mu)&{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\cdot}&{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\cdot}&{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\cdot}\\
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\cdot}&\sin\mu&\sin u&{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\cdot}\\
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\cdot}&\sin u&\sin\mu&{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\cdot}\\
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\cdot}&{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\cdot}&{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\cdot}&\sin(u+\mu)\end{pmatrix}\;,

where the permutation (swap) operator reads

\displaystyle\mathbf{S}=\begin{pmatrix}1&{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\cdot}&{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\cdot}&{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\cdot}\\
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\cdot}&{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\cdot}&1&{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\cdot}\\
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\cdot}&1&{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\cdot}&{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\cdot}\\
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\cdot}&{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\cdot}&{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\cdot}&1\end{pmatrix}\;.

The
\check{R}
-matrix satisfies the circuit-type YBE (
16
),

(37)

\displaystyle\check{\mathbf{R}}_{12}(u-v;\mu)\check{\mathbf{R}}_{23}(u;\mu)\check{\mathbf{R}}_{12}(v;\mu)=\check{\mathbf{R}}_{23}(v;\mu)\check{\mathbf{R}}_{12}(u;\mu)\check{\mathbf{R}}_{23}(u-v;\mu)\;.

The transfer matrix of the XXZ spin chain (6-vertex model) is a bond-dimension-2 MPO, i.e.

(38)

\displaystyle\mathbf{T}_{\rm 6v}(u,\xi;\mu)=\mathrm{Tr}_{0}\mathbf{M}_{0}(u,\xi;\mu)\;,\quad\mathbf{M}_{0}(u,\xi;\mu)=\left[\prod_{j=L}^{1}\mathbf{R}_{0j}(u-\xi_{j};\mu)\right]\;,

acting on the Hilbert space
V_{1}\otimes V_{2}\otimes\cdots\otimes V_{L}=(\mathbb{C}^{2})^{\otimes L}
, after taking the partial trace over the auxiliary space
V_{0}=\mathbb{C}^{2}
.
Here we introduce the inhomogeneities as
\xi=(\xi_{1},\xi_{2},\dots,\xi_{L})\in\mathbb{C}^{L}
. The operator
\mathbf{M}_{0}\in\mathrm{End}(V_{0}\otimes(\mathbb{C}^{2})^{\otimes L})
acting on both the auxiliary 2-dimensional vector space and physical Hilbert space is called the monodromy matrix. By using the YBE recursively, we have

(39)

\displaystyle\mathbf{R}_{ab}(u-v;\mu)\mathbf{M}_{a}(u,\xi;\mu)\mathbf{M}_{b}(v,\xi;\mu)=\mathbf{M}_{b}(v,\xi;\mu)\mathbf{M}_{a}(u,\xi;\mu)\mathbf{R}_{ab}(u-v;\mu)\;.

When
\mathbf{R}_{ab}(u;\mu)
is invertible (it is not invertible only for a few individual values of
u
), upon inverting it and taking the partial trace over the auxiliary spaces
a
and
b
, we have

(40)

\displaystyle\left[\mathbf{T}_{\rm 6v}(u,\xi;\mu),\mathbf{T}_{\rm 6v}(v,\xi;\mu)\right]=0\;.

For the rest of the article, we will take the homogeneous limit, i.e.
\xi_{j}=0
, for all
j\in\{1,2,\cdots,L\}
. In the inhomogeneous case most of the derivation follows the same procedure as well. We will denote the homogeneous transfer matrix with
\xi_{j}=0
as
\mathbf{T}_{\rm 6v}(u;\mu)
.

The XXZ spin chain Hamiltonian is obtained by taking the logarithmic derivative of the transfer matrix evaluated at
u=0
, i.e.

(41)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathbf{H}_{\rm XXZ}&=\left.\sin\mu\,\partial_{u}\log\mathbf{T}_{\rm 6v}(u;\mu)\right|_{u=0}=\sum_{j=1}^{L}\check{\mathbf{R}}_{j,j+1}^{\prime}(0;\mu)\\
&=\sum_{j=1}^{L}\frac{1}{2}\left(X_{j}X_{j+1}+Y_{j}Y_{j+1}+\Delta(\mathbb{I}+Z_{j}Z_{j+1})\right)=\sum_{j=1}^{L}\mathbf{h}_{j,j+1}\;,\end{split}

where the Pauli operators
X_{j}
,
Y_{j}
and
Z_{j}
act non-trivially on
V_{j}
. The anisotropy parameter
\Delta=\cos\mu
is defined as above.

A crucial point is that the
\check{R}
-matrix and the XXZ Hamiltonian can be expressed in terms of a representation of the chromatic algebra mentioned above, with the generators

(42)

\displaystyle S_{j}\mapsto\mathbf{S}_{j}=\frac{1}{2}\left(X_{j}X_{j+1}+Y_{j}Y_{j+1}\right)\;,\quad P_{j}\mapsto\mathbf{P}_{j}=\frac{1}{2}\left(\mathbb{I}+Z_{j}Z_{j+1}\right)\;.

Therefore, we rewrite the
\check{R}
-matrix in terms of the chromatic algebra generators as

(43)

\displaystyle\check{\mathbf{R}}_{j,j+1}(u,\mu)=\sin(u+\mu)\mathbb{I}+\left(\sin(u+\mu)-\sin\mu\right)\mathbf{P}_{j}+\sin u\,\mathbf{S}_{j}\;,\quad\mathbf{h}_{j,j+1}=\mathbf{S}_{j}+\Delta\mathbf{P}_{j}\;.

5.
Case study 1: the cluster entangler applied to the XXZ chain

In this section we consider the SPT-entangler for the cluster spin-chain, given by

\displaystyle U_{c}=U_{c}^{\dagger}=\prod_{j=1}^{L}CZ_{j,j+1}\qquad\mathrm{where}\qquad CZ=\left(\begin{array}[]{cccc}1&{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\cdot}&{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\cdot}&{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\cdot}\\
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\cdot}&1&{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\cdot}&{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\cdot}\\
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\cdot}&{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\cdot}&1&{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\cdot}\\
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\cdot}&{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\cdot}&{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\cdot}&-1\end{array}\right)\ .

We take periodic boundary conditions, identifying site indices
j
with
j+L
.
This is a Clifford transformation that acts as
U_{c}Z_{j}U_{c}^{\dagger}=Z_{j}
and
U_{c}X_{j}U_{c}^{\dagger}=Z_{j-1}X_{j}Z_{j+1}
. Hence, it transforms the trivial paramagnet
\mathbf{H}_{0}=-\sum_{j}X_{j}
to
\mathbf{H}_{\mathrm{cluster}}=-\sum_{j}Z_{j-1}X_{j}Z_{j+1}
.

Of course, we can use it to transform any integrable model. For definiteness, we consider the XXZ spin chain introduced above, giving

(48)

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathbf{H}}_{\mathrm{XXZ}}=U_{c}\mathbf{H}_{\mathrm{XXZ}}U_{c}^{\dagger}=\sum_{\mathrm{sites}~j}\left(Z_{j-1}X_{j}X_{j+1}Z_{j+2}+Z_{j-1}Y_{j}Y_{j+1}Z_{j+2}+\Delta(\mathbb{I}+Z_{j}Z_{j+1})\right)\ .

For
\Delta<1
, this model is at a transition between two distinct ferromagnetically ordered phases
[
5
]
, where the ground state is also an SPT for the unbroken symmetry group
[
55
,
56
]
.
In particular, in the absence of an external field, the XXZ chain has symmetries
\prod_{j}Z_{j}
,
\prod_{j}X_{j}
along with time-reversal. The latter acts as complex conjugation,
\mathcal{K}
, in the
Z
basis. Perturbing
\mathbf{H}_{\mathrm{XXZ}}
with
\delta(X_{j}X_{j+1}-Y_{j}Y_{j+1})
takes us to an
X
or
Y
ordered ferromagnetic phase for
\delta>0
and
\delta<0
respectively. Applying the entangler, the
X
-ferromagnet transforms to a state with long-range order in
Z_{j-1}X_{j}Z_{j+1}
. This implies symmetry fractionalisation of the preserved
\prod_{j}X_{j}
and
\mathcal{K}
symmetries. This is in line with our motivation of using dualities to construct integrable transitions between SPT phases.

The transformation
U_{c}
is itself an MPU with the following decomposition
5
5

5

From this point on, following the conventions of the MPO literature, we read the virtual indices of the MPO tensors from right to left, and not from left to right.
of its square

(49)

Here
\bullet
indicates a
Z
operator, and the
\left|v_{j}\right>
are orthonormal (these vectors are given explicitly in Ref.
[
53
, Eq. 15]
). This is a ‘fixed point form’ for the MPU.
In particular, it satisfies the following fixed-point equations

(50)

These equations can be verified for this particular MPU, but hold more generally for any MPU (after blocking sites, and where
\left|v_{0}\right>
and
\left<v_{0}\right|
replaced by appropriate vectors)
[
52
,
53
]
. See also
Section

7.1.1
. These equations imply the ‘pulling through’ equation

(51)

and its mirror image.

The transfer matrix
\tilde{T}(u)=\mathbf{U}_{c}T(u)\mathbf{U}_{c}
for
\tilde{H}_{\mathrm{XXZ}}
is naturally constructed from Lax operators
\tilde{L}(u)=U_{c}L(u)U_{c}
(we suppress all indices, see also the diagram (
52
)). Globally the transfer matrices
\tilde{T}(u)
commute for different values of the spectral parameter, since they are unitarily equivalent to
T
. However, since both the MPU and the
T
are MPOs that are built from local tensors, one might hope there is a local relation that gives the commutation of
\tilde{T}
. Of course, this is not guaranteed on general grounds. In the next subsection we explain how we can define an extended
R
matrix, following
[
17
]
, that provides such a local relation.

5.1.
The modified RLL relation

We cannot immediately substitute the XXZ
R
-matrix into a local RLL relation, due to the
U_{c}
tensors. However, the pulling through equation is suggestive that projecting onto
\left|v_{0}\right>
on the virtual space is helpful. Let us consider the local RLL relation (
14
), where the
R
-matrix is extended by tensoring with this projector and identity operators (see
Eq.

52
). After simple manipulations given in
Appendix

B
, this reduces to

(52)

If the extended
R
-matrix were a true intertwiner then the right-hand-side would vanish. Nevertheless this local relationship is enough to see that the transfer matrices commute; the right-hand-side vanishes as soon as we take additional
\tilde{L}
operators to the left and right. Including these operators gives us the ‘modified YBA’ of Ref.
[
17
]
, that can be used to prove commutativity of the transfer matrices directly. This is given by

(53)

where the
L
operators to the left and right may depend on arbitrary spectral parameter. Note that if we consider the dual
\tilde{L}=U_{c}LU_{c}
as a single tensor, then this identity shows that the extended
R
-matrix acts as an intertwiner in the presence of additional
\tilde{L}
.

6.
Case study 2: vertex-face correspondence and Kramers–Wannier

In addition to those transformations, like the MPU above, where local spin operators are always mapped into local spin operators, we are interested in the family of
non-invertible
MPO transformation that is motivated by the gauging of global symmetries of finite group type
[
57
]
. Such MPOs have an interpretation as maps between different module categories of a certain fusion category
[
58
,
54
]
.

We note that the non-invertible MPOs we consider will not map every local spin operator to a local one. Locality is preserved only for operators that preserve the discrete global symmetry. Therefore, it makes sense to consider an integrable Hamiltonian with a particular discrete global symmetry and its ‘interaction’ with the non-invertible MPO that gauges this symmetry.

The
\check{R}
-matrix of an integrable Hamiltonian with a discrete global symmetry transforms naturally under non-invertible duality. After the duality transformation, the dual Hamiltonian, still in terms of local operators, has the integrable structure from the dual
\check{R}
-matrix that still satisfies the Yang–Baxter equations. We exemplify this property in the following discussion.

We focus on the non-invertible MPO that implements the renowned Kramer–Wannier (KW) duality of the Ising model
[
13
]
. This corresponds to gauging the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{z}
symmetry generated by the operator

(54)

\displaystyle\eta=\prod_{j=1}^{L}Z_{j}\;.

We will comment on more general cases in the next section. Here we show how the integrable structures of the XXZ spin chain map onto the corresponding structures in the Ising zig-zag model under the KW duality
[
16
,
59
,
60
,
61
,
50
]
.

The KW MPO is a bond-dimension two MPO that can be expressed as

(55)

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}_{\rm KW}=\mathrm{Tr}_{a}\prod_{j=1}^{L}\left(\mathbf{A}_{a,j-1/2}\mathbf{B}_{a,j}\right)\;,

and which can be depicted by the diagram

(56)

The KW MPO realises the gauging of the global
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
symmetry
\eta
, which in terms of operators defined on the Hilbert space
(\mathbb{C}^{2})^{\otimes L}
means

(59)

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\mathcal{D}_{\rm KW}^{\dagger}\mathcal{D}_{\rm KW}^{\vphantom{\dagger}}=\mathbb{I}+\prod_{j=1}^{L}Z_{j}=\mathbb{I}+\eta\;,\\
&\mathcal{D}_{\rm KW}^{\vphantom{\dagger}}\mathcal{D}_{\rm KW}^{\dagger}=\mathbb{I}+\prod_{j=1}^{L}Z_{j-1/2}=\mathbb{I}+\tilde{\eta}\;,\end{split}

where
\tilde{\eta}
is the global
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
symmetry of the dual model
6
6

6

In general for a model with global finite group symmetry
G
, the dual model has the global symmetry
\mathrm{Rep}(G)
, i.e. the representation category of
G
. For finite Abelian group,
\mathrm{Rep}(G)\simeq G
when there is no anomaly. This holds for the KW MPO transformation.
.

The KW MPO satisfies the following properties,

(60)

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}_{\rm KW}Z_{n}=X_{n-1/2}X_{n+1/2}\mathcal{D}_{\rm KW}\;,\quad\mathcal{D}_{\rm KW}X_{n}X_{n+1}=Z_{n+1/2}\mathcal{D}_{\rm KW}\;.

These equations follow from the local tensors satisfying the relation

Therefore, the
\check{R}
-matrix transforms as follows,

(62)

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}_{\rm KW}\check{\mathbf{R}}_{j,j+1}(u)=\check{\mathsf{R}}_{j-1/2,j+1/2,j+3/2}(u)\mathcal{D}_{\rm KW}\;,

where the left-hand and right-hand sides are shown diagrammatically as

(63)

\displaystyle\raisebox{-0.5pt}{

\check{\mathsf{R}}

respectively.

The dual
\check{R}
-matrix reads

(65)

\displaystyle\check{\mathsf{R}}_{j-1/2,j+1/2,j+3/2}(u)=\mathbb{I}+\tilde{\mathbf{P}}_{j}+\frac{\sin u}{\sin(u+\mu)}\tilde{\mathbf{S}}_{j}-\frac{\sin\mu}{\sin(u+\mu)}\tilde{\mathbf{P}}_{j}\;.

The operators
\tilde{\mathbf{S}}_{j}
and
\tilde{\mathbf{P}}_{j}
form a different representation of the same chromatic algebra as the XXZ model,

(66)

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathbf{S}}_{j}=\frac{1}{2}Z_{j+1/2}\left(\mathbb{I}-X_{j-1/2}X_{j+3/2}\right)\;,\quad\tilde{\mathbf{P}}_{j}=\frac{1}{2}\left(\mathbb{I}+X_{j-1/2}X_{j+3/2}\right)\;.

Since the
\check{R}
-matrix of the XXZ model satisfies the Yang–Baxter equation (
16
) from the baxterization of the chromatic algebra, the
\check{R}
-matrix of the dual model also satisfies the Yang–Baxter equation (
16
),

\displaystyle\check{\mathsf{R}}_{j-\frac{1}{2},j+\frac{1}{2},j+\frac{3}{2}}(u-v)

\displaystyle\check{\mathsf{R}}_{j+\frac{1}{2},j+\frac{3}{2},j+\frac{5}{2}}(u)\check{\mathsf{R}}_{j-\frac{1}{2},j+\frac{1}{2},j+\frac{3}{2}}(v)

(68)

\displaystyle=\check{\mathsf{R}}_{j+\frac{1}{2},j+\frac{3}{2},j+\frac{5}{2}}(v)\check{\mathsf{R}}_{j-\frac{1}{2},j+\frac{1}{2},j+\frac{3}{2}}(u)\check{\mathsf{R}}_{j+\frac{1}{2},j+\frac{3}{2},j+\frac{5}{2}}(u-v)\;.

Following the argument of the baxterization, we write down two Hamiltonians from the
\check{R}
-matrices,

(69)

\displaystyle\mathbf{H}_{\rm XXZ}=\sum_{j=1}\check{\mathbf{R}}_{j,j+1}^{\prime}(0)\;,\quad\mathbf{H}_{\rm Izz}=\sum_{j=1}\check{\mathsf{R}}_{j-1/2,j+1/2,j+3/2}^{\prime}(0)\;.

Specifically, the XXZ Hamiltonian is given in (
41
), while the Ising zig-zag Hamiltonian is

(70)

\displaystyle\mathbf{H}_{\rm Izz}=\sum_{j=1}^{L}Z_{j+1/2}(\mathbb{I}-X_{j-1/2}X_{j+3/2})+\Delta(\mathbb{I}+X_{j-1/2}X_{j+3/2})\;.

The next step is to construct the transfer matrix for the dual model (
70
). Similar to the vertex model scenario, by rotating the
\check{R}
-matrix by
\frac{\pi}{4}
, we get the corresponding
R
-matrix, i.e.

(71)

In terms of the language of integrability, the transfer matrix
\mathbf{T}_{\rm Izz}
is a transfer matrix of a face-type model, compared to the transfer matrix of the vertex-type
\mathbf{T}_{\rm XXZ}
. Therefore, the duality transformation
\mathcal{D}_{\rm KW}
can be seen as a vertex-face correspondence, similar to the one of Baxter between 6-vertex model and the SOS model
[
22
,
23
,
24
]
.

The transfer matrix (
72
) is
regular
, i.e.

(73)

\displaystyle\mathbf{T}_{\rm Izz}(0)=\quad\raisebox{-0.5pt}{

Therefore, the transfer matrices of the dual model commute with each other using the same ‘train argument’ as in the vertex model case,

(75)

\displaystyle\left[\mathbf{T}_{\rm Izz}(u),\mathbf{T}_{\rm Izz}(v)\right]=0\;,\quad\forall u,v\in\mathbb{C}\;.

We justify the integrability of the Hamiltonian (
70
) by taking the logarithmic derivatives of the transfer matrix (
72
), which is obtained in the same way as the vertex model case (i.e. the XXZ Hamiltonian),

(76)

\displaystyle\mathbf{H}_{\rm Izz}=\left.\sin\mu\,\partial_{u}\mathbf{T}_{\rm Izz}(u)\right|_{u=0}=\sum_{n=1}^{L}\check{\mathsf{R}}_{n-1,n,n+1}^{\prime}(0)\;,

since the Hamiltonian commutes with the transfer matrix,

(77)

\displaystyle\left[\mathbf{H}_{\rm Izz},\mathbf{T}_{\rm Izz}(u)\right]=0\;,\quad\forall u\in\mathbb{C}\;.

Here we would like to point out that the transfer matrix (
72
) is defined in a similar manner to the
split-index matrix product operator
, whose definitions and properties can be found in
[
62
]
.

The KW duality operator not only intertwines the two
\check{R}
-matrices of the XXZ model and its dual, but also their transfer matrices,

(78)

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}_{\rm KW}\mathbf{T}_{\rm 6v}(u)=\mathbf{T}_{\rm Izz}(u)\mathcal{D}_{\rm KW}\;.

Unlike the duality transformation of the
\check{R}
-matrices (
62
) that can be proven using the property of the duality operator, we are only able to prove the duality transformation of the transfer matrices (
78
) by expanding the logarithm of the transfer matrices order by order, i.e.

(79)

\displaystyle\log\mathbf{T}_{\rm 6v}(u)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{u^{n}}{n!}\mathbf{Q}_{n},\quad\log\mathbf{T}_{\rm Izz}(u)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{u^{n}}{n!}\mathsf{Q}_{n}\;,

where

(80)

\displaystyle\mathbf{Q}_{n}=\sum_{j}f_{n}(\check{\mathbf{R}}_{j,j+1}),\quad\mathsf{Q}_{n}=\sum_{j}f_{n}(\check{\mathsf{R}}_{j-1/2,j+1/2,j+3/2})\;.

It is straightforward to verify that

(81)

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}_{\rm KW}\mathbf{Q}_{n}=\mathsf{Q}_{n}\mathcal{D}_{\rm KW}\;,\quad\forall n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}\;,

using (
62
).

Therefore, we arrive at (
78
), showing the relations between the transfer matrices of the XXZ spin chain and its dual model. This derivation also elucidates the integrability of the dual Ising zig-zag Hamiltonian (
70
).

7.
MPO transformed lattice models—general observations

In this section we discuss how the case studies reflect general principles.
Let us first collect some useful results about MPOs and MPUs.

7.1.
Properties of MPO that have an exact MPO inverse

Let us consider invertible MPOs where the inverse is another MPO with finite bond dimension
7
7

7

This is a restriction: there exist examples of invertible MPO where the inverse is not an MPO
[
63
,
64
]
. One way to see this is that the MPO transfer matrix in the references leads to quasilocal charges, a contradiction to the result of
Section

7.1.2
.
. This class includes all MPUs as a subfamily.

We restrict to translation-invariant MPO, defined by a single tensor
A\in\mathcal{M}_{D}\otimes\mathcal{M}_{d}
(
\mathcal{M}_{D}
denotes the vector space of
D\times D
matrices),

(82)

\displaystyle A=\sum_{i,j=1}^{d}A_{ij}\otimes\left|i\right>\left<j\right|\ .

The MPO on
n
sites is an operator
O_{n}(A)\in\mathcal{M}_{d}^{\otimes n}
defined as

(83)

\displaystyle O_{n}(A)=\sum_{\mathbf{i},\mathbf{j}}\mathrm{tr}(A_{i_{1},j_{1}}A_{i_{2},j_{2}}\dots A_{i_{n},j_{n}})\left|i_{1}i_{2}\dots i_{n}\right>\left<j_{1}j_{2}\dots j_{n}\right|.

Graphically, this operator is represented as

Note that considering MPOs that are invertible and whose inverse is also an MPO means that we assume that for the MPO tensor
A\in\mathcal{M}_{D}\otimes\mathcal{M}_{d}
there is another MPO tensor
B\in\mathcal{M}_{D^{\prime}}\otimes\mathcal{M}_{d}
such that
O_{n}(A)\cdot O_{n}(B)=O_{n}(B)\cdot O_{n}(A)=\mathbb{I}^{\otimes n}
.

Given this set up we have the following results, proved in
Appendix

A
. While the proofs follow relatively straightforwardly from existing literature
[
65
]
, we expect these results to be of independent interest.

This gives the pulling through equation (
51
) for such MPO if we block the physical sites.

7.1.2.
Locality preservation

Consider now any local operator
X
, supported on
k
sites, and conjugate by the MPO
O_{n}(A)
(where
n
is sufficiently large). We have that

(91)

\displaystyle O_{n}(A)XO_{n}(A)^{-1}=O_{n}(A)XO_{n}(B)

is a local operator, and the size of its support is
k+2n_{0}
(
n_{0}
is the nilpotency length (
87
)). This generalises the result that MPU are equivalent to quantum cellular automata
[
52
,
53
]
.

7.1.3.
Generator of local charges

The logarithmic derivative of an MPO with MPO inverse is local. To see this, we further assume that the MPO tensor depends (differentiably) on a parameter
t
:
A=A_{t}
, and for all
t
there is an inverse MPO described by the tensor
B_{t}
. We have that the operator

(92)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}O_{n}(A_{t})\cdot O_{n}(B_{t})

is a sum of terms, where each term is at most
(2n_{0}+1)
-local. Here
n_{0}
is the nilpotency length associated with the
A
-
B
transfer matrix at the fixed time
t
.

N
annihilates the dominant left-right eigenvectors

B=\overline{A}

7.2.
The extended
R
-matrix and modified RLL relation

If we transform a Yang–Baxter-integrable lattice model by conjugating by an MPO that has an exact MPO inverse, we can reason as in
Section

5
to arrive at the modified RLL relation.

Eq.

84
. If we define transformed Lax operators
\tilde{L}=BLA

allows us to repeat the reasoning given in

Note that, as discussed in
[
17
]
for the MPU case, the extended
R
matrix (including the projector) satisfies the YBE. It is not, however, a regular solution. In particular, it is not invertible. This non-invertibility causes no issue in the argument for commuting transfer matrices due to the structure of the MPO. The argument itself uses the modified YBA (see
Eq.

53
which is easily generalised), and is clearest if we first block physical sites such that the effective nilpotency length is one.

7.3.
Obstructions in the MPU case and charge pumps

Suppose that the right-hand-side of
Eq.

94
vanishes, meaning that our extended
R
matrix satisfies a standard RLL relation. The simplest way this can happen is when
\tilde{N}_{R}=\tilde{N}_{L}=0
. If our MPO is an MPU, then, from the argument in
Section

7.1.4
, this implies that the MPU must be bond dimension one. As discussed in
Section

3.1
this trivially preserves the integrable structure.

This observation does not rule out more subtle ways that the usual RLL equation can be obtained for the extended
R
matrix. For example, the contraction of the Lax operators with
\tilde{N}_{R}
could vanish. We verify that this does not occur generically in the XXZ model case in
Appendix

B
.

Another interesting point is the connection of the obstructions
\tilde{N}_{L/R}
to charge pumps.
From
Section

5
, the decomposition
Eq.

49
makes clear that
\mathrm{tr}(M_{1}M_{2}\dots M_{k})=\mathbb{I}
for any
k
(of course, this can be interpreted as the MPU generating a unitary for any system size). The trace is essential, since

(96)

\displaystyle M_{1}\dots M_{k}=\left|v_{0}\right>\left<v_{0}\right|\mathbb{I}+\left|v_{0}\right>\left<v_{2}\right|Z_{k}+\left|v_{1}\right>\left<v_{0}\right|Z_{1}+\left|v_{1}\right>\left<v_{2}\right|Z_{1}Z_{k}\ .

Without the trace, this operator is a variant of
\mathbf{U}_{c}
that depends on the boundary termination on the virtual space. Let
\mathbf{U}^{\textrm{OBC}}_{c}
be
\mathbf{U}_{c}
on an open chain, where we remove the operator
CZ_{L,1}
that connects the two ends. Fixing
\left<v_{0}\right|M_{1}M_{2}\dots M_{k}\left|v_{0}\right>
amounts to applying
\mathbf{U}_{c}^{\textrm{OBC}}(\mathbf{U}^{\textrm{OBC}}_{c})^{-1}=\mathbb{I}
.
On the other hand
\left<v_{1}\right|M_{1}M_{2}\dots M_{k}\left|v_{2}\right>
corresponds to applying
\left(\mathbf{U}_{c}^{\textrm{OBC}}\right)^{2}=Z_{1}Z_{L}
. This can be interpreted as a charge pump for the
\prod_{j}X_{j}
symmetry that is preserved when we map
\mathbf{H}_{0}
to
\mathbf{H}_{\mathrm{cluster}}

[
25
,
66
]
. The charged operator,
Z
, obstructs our local RLL relation
Eq.

52
, but must appear so that we have a possible boundary termination of
Eq.

96
such that we recover the pump behaviour of
\left(\mathbf{U}_{c}^{\textrm{OBC}}\right)^{2}
.

Given our motivation of using MPU transformations to change symmetry properties of our integrable model, we expect this behaviour to be typical. Indeed, following the discussion in
[
66
]
, we expect that SPT entanglers will often give rise to charge pumps, and thus in this important class of examples the nilpotent operator
N
will have some symmetry charge. It would be interesting to explore this further, and to understand how this charge may constrain the modified RLL relation.

7.4.
The non-invertible case

Here we make some general remarks on the non-invertible duality over integrable models.
The construction of
Section

6
can be generalized to other non-invertible duality transformations that correspond to the gauging of certain discrete symmetries of the Hamiltonian (and the
R
-matrix), where the integrability is still preserved in the dual model, while some global symmetries might differ
[
57
,
67
]
. The dual model possesses symmetries described by
\mathrm{Rep}(G)
, which can be non-invertible when
G
is non-Abelian.
Alternatively, integrable models with non-invertible categorical symmetry can be built using the method outlined in
[
16
,
50
,
58
,
54
]
. It would be interesting to compare the results using both approaches. The baxterization process indicates that the integrability structure remains the same in the bulk after the duality transformation. Meanwhile, the boundary conditions that are compatible with the non-invertible duality are given by the categorical data of the gauged discrete group
G
, which
a priori
do not necessarily preserve the integrability. We do not elaborate on this issue in the current paper, analysis is deferred to future work.

Another useful observation is to understand the non-invertible duality transformation between two integrable models in their field theory limit. This can be understood as orbifolding
[
7
]
. For example, the XXZ spin chain Hamiltonian (
41
) in the regime
|\Delta|\leq 1
flows to the
c=1
free compactified boson CFT with radius
r=\sqrt{\frac{\pi}{2(\pi-\arccos\Delta)}}

[
6
,
7
]
. The dual model (
70
) thus flows to the same
c=1
CFT with
r^{\prime}=\frac{r}{2}
after the
U(1)/\mathbb{Z}_{2}
orbifolding
8
8

8

Note that this orbifolding procedure does
not
correspond to the orbifold branch of the
c=1
moduli space
[
7
]
. If we instead gauge the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{x}
symmetry of the XXZ spin chain in the manner described in
Section

6
, we shall arrive at the orbifold branch with the same radius in the CFT limit. See also the discussion in
[
68
]
.
. A similar check can be performed by analysing the partition functions of the corresponding vertex models after inserting the non-invertible duality defects
[
50
]
.

8.
Outlook

In this work we analysed MPO dualities between integrable lattice models. For MPO with exact MPO inverse, we found that the MPO-dual
\check{R}
matrix continues to satisfy the circuit YBE and therefore may be used in the baxterization approach. Care must be taken with boundary conditions in this case. For Yang–Baxter integrable models, we found that generically neither the
R
-matrix nor the local RLL equation is preserved. However, we could find a canonical extended
R
-matrix (using a projector derived from the MPO) that gives rise to a modified RLL equation and Yang–Baxter algebra. This allows for a local derivation of commuting transfer matrices in the dual model, and elucidates the integrable structure. In the case of non-invertible MPOs we found that the integrable structure of the dual model remains the same by virtue of the baxterization, and is reminiscent of the renowned vertex-face correspondence. We wrote down explicitly the transfer matrix of the dual model as a split-index MPO for the example of applying the KW duality to the XXZ spin chain.

For MPU dualities the analysis ended up being identical to the more general case of an MPO with exact MPO inverse. It would be interesting to identify special classes of unitaries where the dualities are simplified, but not so simple as the on-site case. While we excluded one route to simplification above, it would be worth exploring the case of dual unitary transformations
[
69
]
.

For non-invertible MPOs we focused on the case of discrete gauging. It would be very interesting to understand broader classes of models which could give useful, physically motivated, dualities of integrable models. It would be useful to understand more deeply the connection between classification of medium-range integrable models
[
61
]
and those that arise by MPO transformation of nearest-neighbour models.

More broadly, it would be very interesting to utilise modern tensor network techniques to gain a better understanding of the field of integrability. Exciting recent work in this direction
[
70
]
that showed the XYZ spin chain is integrable via a tensor network approach, avoiding complications of the standard method, is encouraging.
Results for MPO with exact MPO inverse that we include above, such as locality of the logarithmic derivatives, may be useful in numerical approaches.

, taking the setup and statements in that section as a given.

where we have used that the single term containing only the tensor
N
disappears, as it contains the contraction of
n-k>n_{0}
tensors, which is zero. To draw the diagram, we also assumed that
n=l_{0}+k+r_{0}
, the operator
X
is located at sites
(l_{0}+1),\dots,(l_{0}+k)
, and that
l_{0}>n
and
r_{0}>n
(that is,
n
is large enough and that
X
is somewhere in the ‘middle’). The resulting operator is clearly local: all the terms in the sum are localized between the sites
l_{0}-n_{0}
and
l_{0}+k+n_{0}
.

A.3.
Generator of local charges

Similar to the argument above, we can show that the logarithmic derivative of such an MPO is local.

O_{n}(A)

Let us consider this equation with the boundary
\mathbb{I}\otimes X
, where
\mathbb{I}
acts on the upper virtual index, and
X
is an arbitrary matrix acting on the bottom virtual index. We obtain

(103)

\displaystyle U_{n}^{\dagger}\cdot U_{n}(X)=\mathrm{tr}(LRX)\cdot\mathbb{I}^{\otimes n}\ ,

where

(104)

\displaystyle U_{n}(X)=\sum_{ij}\mathrm{tr}(XU_{i_{1},j_{1}}U_{i_{2},j_{2}}\dots U_{i_{n},j_{n}})\left|i_{1}i_{2}\dots i_{n}\right>\left<j_{1}j_{2}\dots j_{n}\right|\ ,

and thus
U_{n}(X)\propto U_{n}
for all
X
. As
U
is an injective tensor, this implies that its bond dimension is one, and thus
U_{n}=u^{\otimes n}
for a local unitary
u
.

Appendix B
The transformed RLL relation

In this appendix we analyse the local RLL relation for an MPU transformed model after including the projector onto
\left|v_{0}\right>
in the virtual space. We use the particular expansion given in
Eq.

49
for the cluster entangler, but this is representative of the general case.

(105)

In the first term on the right-hand-side, we can now use the standard RLL relation (
14
). The second term contains a
Z
operator, and there is no reason that
R(u,v)
acts as an intertwiner in such a case (and for the XXZ model it does not, see below for the explicit calculation). We thus have that

(106)

Analysing the right-hand-side of the RLL relation, we have that

(107)

taking the difference gives
Eq.

52
.

Since the two vectors
|v_{0}\rangle
and
|v_{2}\rangle
are orthogonal, the right hand-side of
Eq.

52
is independent of the left-hand side. Moreover, we expect that each side of
Eq.

52
is non-vanishing for any
u,v\in\mathbb{C}
. In the XXZ case, we have that

(108)

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}M_{1}&{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\cdot}&{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\cdot}&{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\cdot}&{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\cdot}&{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\cdot}&{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\cdot}&{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\cdot}\\
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\cdot}&M_{2}&M_{3}&{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\cdot}&M_{4}&{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\cdot}&{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\cdot}&{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\cdot}\\
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\cdot}&M_{5}&M_{6}&{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\cdot}&M_{7}&{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\cdot}&{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\cdot}&{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\cdot}\\
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\cdot}&{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\cdot}&{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\cdot}&M_{8}&{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\cdot}&M_{9}&M_{10}&{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\cdot}\\
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\cdot}&M_{11}&M_{12}&{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\cdot}&M_{13}&{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\cdot}&{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\cdot}&{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\cdot}\\
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\cdot}&{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\cdot}&{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\cdot}&M_{14}&{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\cdot}&M_{15}&M_{16}&{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\cdot}\\
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\cdot}&{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\cdot}&{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\cdot}&M_{17}&{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\cdot}&M_{18}&M_{19}&{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\cdot}\\
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\cdot}&{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\cdot}&{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\cdot}&{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\cdot}&{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\cdot}&{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\cdot}&{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\cdot}&-M_{1}\end{pmatrix}\;,

where the matrix elements are given explicitly as

(109)

\displaystyle\begin{split}&M_{1}=\sin(u-v+\mu)\sin(u+\mu)\sin(v+\mu)\;,\\
&M_{2}=-\sin u\,\sin v\sin(u-v+\mu)\;,\quad M_{3}=-\sin u\,\sin\mu\sin(u-v+\mu)\;,\\
&M_{4}=\sin\mu\,\sin(v+\mu)\sin(u-v+\mu)\;,\\
&M_{5}=\sin\mu\,(\sin(u+\mu)\sin(u-v)-\sin\mu\,\sin v)\;,\\
&M_{6}=\sin v\,\sin(u+\mu)\sin(u-v)-\sin^{3}\mu\;,\\
&M_{7}=\sin\mu\,\sin u\,\sin(v+\mu)\;,\quad M_{8}=-\sin u\,\sin(u-v)\sin(v+\mu)\;,\\
&M_{9}=\sin\mu(\sin\mu\,\sin(u-v)-\sin v\,\sin(u+\mu))\;,\\
&M_{10}=\sin\mu(\sin v\,\sin(u-v)-\sin\mu\,\sin(u+\mu))\;,\\
&M_{11}=\sin\mu(\sin\mu\,\sin(u+\mu)-\sin v\,\sin(u-v))\;,\\
&M_{12}=\sin\mu(\sin v\,\sin(u+\mu)-\sin\mu\sin(u-v))\;,\\
&M_{13}=\sin u\,\sin(u-v)\sin(v+\mu)\;,\quad M_{14}=-\sin u\,\sin\mu\,\sin(v+\mu)\;,\\
&M_{15}=\sin^{3}\mu-\sin v\sin(u+\mu)\sin(u-v)\;,\\
&M_{16}=\sin\mu(\sin\mu\,\sin v-\sin(u+\mu)\sin(u-v))\;,\\
&M_{17}=-\sin\mu\,\sin(v+\mu)\sin(u-v+\mu)\;,\\
&M_{18}=\sin\mu\,\sin u\,\sin(u-v+\mu)\;,\quad M_{19}=\sin u\,\sin v\,\sin(u-v+\mu)\;.\end{split}

(Recall that each index in the diagram corresponds to a two-dimensional space. For the matrix representation the row corresponds to grouping anticlockwise the left virtual indices and the bottom physical index, while the column corresponds to grouping anticlockwise the remaining indices.)

Meanwhile, the term

(110)

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}M_{1}&{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\cdot}&{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\cdot}&{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\cdot}&{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\cdot}&{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\cdot}&{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\cdot}&{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\cdot}\\
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\cdot}&M_{2}&M_{3}&{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\cdot}&M_{4}&{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\cdot}&{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\cdot}&{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\cdot}\\
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\cdot}&M_{5}&M_{6}&{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\cdot}&M_{7}&{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\cdot}&{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\cdot}&{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\cdot}\\
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\cdot}&{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\cdot}&{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\cdot}&M_{8}&{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\cdot}&M_{9}&M_{10}&{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\cdot}\\
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\cdot}&M_{11}&M_{12}&{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\cdot}&M_{13}&{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\cdot}&{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\cdot}&{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\cdot}\\
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\cdot}&{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\cdot}&{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\cdot}&M_{14}&{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\cdot}&M_{15}&M_{16}&{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\cdot}\\
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\cdot}&{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\cdot}&{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\cdot}&M_{17}&{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\cdot}&M_{18}&M_{19}&{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\cdot}\\
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\cdot}&{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\cdot}&{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\cdot}&{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\cdot}&{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\cdot}&{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\cdot}&{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\cdot}&-M_{1}\end{pmatrix}^{\mathrm{T}}\;,

which implies that for generic values of
u,v\in\mathbb{C}
, we have that

(111)

\displaystyle\vbox{\hbox{
\includegraphics[]{Eq103.pdf}}}-\vbox{\hbox{
\includegraphics[]{Eq105.pdf}}}\neq 0\;.

Therefore, at least for the XXZ case,
Eq.

52
is non-vanishing for generic values of
u,v\in\mathbb{C}
.
